#6985. Your December Horoscope by Zodiac Sign

We’re wrapping up the year with great momentum going into the new year.

December’s astrology helps you wrap the year up with some forward momentum! At the start of the month, the Sun continues its joyful journey through Sagittarius, putting you in a more adventurous mood and encouraging you to expand your horizons and perspective. It's a great time to get in a debate during a holiday dinner and stand up for what you believe in, but to also practice your active listening skills and remain open to the possibility that you could learn something from someone else. You’re zooming out and thinking more about the world around you and eager to soak up life’s experiences by trying new things and meeting new people.

You’re feeling all-or-nothing when it comes to love once Venus moves into Scorpio on December 4. For the next few weeks, Venus is retracing its steps that it took during the retrograde, so you might finally experience relief or turning points regarding some of your key relationships and contracts. This can help you navigate uncomfortable terrain in your close connections with honesty and vulnerability. You’re feeling funnier than normal once Mercury enters Sagittarius on December 6. When our communication planet moves through jokester Sagittarius, your conversations are a bit more playful but words can also sting from time to time. It’s easier to speak what’s on your mind in the moment and regret it later. You might also have a harder time finding a plan or strategy to reach certain goals, but right now you’re not meant to sort through the details. Focus on the big picture and soaking up new information and experiences instead. If you’re traveling, you might also deal with more snafus, so plan accordingly!

A New Moon in Sagittarius on December 8 helps you reconnect with a source of hope or optimism. Conversations feel expansive and information that you learn might inform your worldview in a new way. This energy is also helpful for reframing the way you speak to yourself and releasing limiting beliefs. You might find yourself eager to book some travel or sign up for a workshop to hone a skill or learn something new. A few days later, on December 12, Neptune stations direct in Aries, helping you to dissolve your ego. This brings you back to July 7, and puts you in touch with certain topics and conversations that were a focus around then. You might feel more tender and sensitive, or feel a burst of creativity. This can put you in a philosophical mood, asking you to reflect on whether you’re living in alignment with your beliefs. There might also be developments regarding teachers and mentors in your life, decisions about going back to school, or turning points regarding some of your goals and dreams.

The Sun enters Capricorn on December 21, marking the Winter Solstice in the Northern Hemisphere and the longest night of the year. From now on, the daylight will slowly begin to increase once again. But first, you must move through winter and preserve your precious energy in the process. This is not the time to overdo it or overextend yourself. It’s time to prune people, patterns, and situations from your life that extinguish your drive and essence instead of replenishing them. A Full Moon in Cancer on December 23, highlights your relationship with the past, primary caregivers, and security. There might be significant developments within your chosen family or perhaps you’re making a decision about where you live. People are in a more sentimental mood and the past can stir up major feelings and reflections. Be gentle with yourself and others.

The month wraps up with Mercury entering Capricorn on December 25, lending you a more structured approach to your ideas and communication style. You might find yourself navigating boundaries more or even find yourself speaking with a little more directness. There’s no need to sugarcoat to keep others comfortable, your time is limited and you’ve got things to do! This energy can be productive, help you problem solve, and stick with habits and plans that build toward your longterm success.

#6979. Claude Intros Sonnet 5.5 with Model-Switching Cybersecurity Safeguards

Anthropic's new Claude release, Sonnet 5.5, brings cybersecurity safeguards that can change which model handles a request during a Claude conversation. Higher-risk tasks can fall back to Sonnet 5.

According to Anthropic's explanation of model switching, automatic fallback is enabled by default in Claude. Users receive a notice, and the answer is labeled with the model that provided it. The chat then stays on Sonnet 5 unless the user changes the selection.

The company identifies penetration testing and exploit generation among activities that may trigger cybersecurity fallbacks. Source-code vulnerability scanning remains supported. Biology and reasoning-extraction safeguards can block requests outright, rather than route them elsewhere.

The distinction matters operationally. A block stops the request; a fallback allows an attempt to continue with a different model. Neither outcome should be treated as interchangeable with completion by the model the user originally chose.

Anthropic says Sonnet 5.5 generates output more than 30% faster than Sonnet 5 and can reduce cost per task by up to 30%, based on its testing. Token prices remain unchanged.

Those claims describe performance under the company's test conditions. An organization evaluating the service also needs to examine what happens when its own assignments encounter safeguards.

An internal pilot could record how often work is interrupted, whether fallback answers are usable, and when users need help. Such measurements would give buyers a more relevant basis for deployment than assuming every task receives the advertised model's capabilities.

Anthropic has announced plans to expand its Cyber Verification Program with tiered access for Sonnet 5.5, Opus 5.5, and Mythos models. Its current program guidance explicitly excludes the two new 5.5 models and says the expansion is forthcoming.

Organizations should distinguish those planned arrangements from access available today.

The broader enterprise issue is accountability. When an AI service changes models during an assignment, someone still needs to determine whether the resulting work is suitable. A visible handoff helps, but organizations must decide how employees should respond to it.

For more information on Sonnet 5.5, go to the Anthropic site.

#6976. Tom Hardy's 5 Most Stylish Characters, Ranked

From Inception's sharply-dressed con-man to the getaway driver in RocknRolla, here’s what you can learn from his on-screen wardrobe.

Tom Hardy doesn’t usually play the good guy. He’s been a forger, a fixer, a getaway driver, a rogue spy, and, in one of his more ambitious films, both halves of the most notorious twins in British crime (not that there’s much competition). Most of them are walking red flags, sure, but they’re red flags in great clothes.

That’s partly down to the costume designers, who’ve given Hardy everything from ’60s East End tailoring to Steve McQueen-inspired ’70s outerwear, but it’s also down to Hardy, who has a particular knack for making a wardrobe feel like his own. Put him in a Western shirt or three-piece and he’ll look equally at home in both (and you’ll want to steal the look, either way).

So, we’ve dug through the archives to rank his best-dressed roles of all time, and one of them’s actually on TV right now. Consider it your new watch list—just ignore the red flags.

Everyone remembers the spinning top and Joseph Gordon-Levitt’s three-piece, but the best-dressed man in Inception is the one who usually gets overlooked: Tom Hardy’s Eames. Costume designer Jeffrey Kurland imagined him as an expat from a Graham Greene novel, and every piece was made from scratch (even though half of it looks straight off a Dries Van Noten runway). Think batik-print shirts, collars spread over relaxed blazers, sharp trousers, and a wallet chain—basically clothes for a guy who spends too long sitting in the sun and definitely doesn’t wear SPF. Then there’s the watch: a gold tank-style antique Kurland picked up from the prop master’s collection—and he’s since forgotten the brand. Reddit’s watch sleuths are still on the case, 16 years and counting.

Legend gave its costume department a very important task: how do you tell Tom Hardy apart from Tom Hardy? Lead designer Caroline Harris and Soho tailor Chris Kerr solved it with buttons: Reggie, the charmer, wears single-breasted suits that feel safe and easy to trust. Ronnie, meanwhile, is the volatile one who gets three-piece double-breasted silhouettes with extra padding to make him look macho. On both sides, though, everything’s cut from vintage fabrics for a lived-in look straight from the hanger. Sixty years on, you could walk into any hotel bar dressed as either and nobody would bat an eye. Until you start talking business, that is.

Inspired by the real-world Cold War scandals that rocked British Intelligence, Tinker Tailor Soldier Spy follows the hunt for a Russian mole inside MI6 in 1973. So, as you might expect, it’s lots of men doing stuff in gray suits. Not Hardy’s Ricki Tarr, though. He’s the only agent with any personality, wardrobe-wise, all thanks to costume designer Jacqueline Durran. Her thinking? Steve McQueen on a mission: a shearling coat in Istanbul, a Baracuta Harrington in Paris, and not a standard suit in sight. The kind of menswear every guy on your FYP is wearing today—Hardy was just 15 years early.

In MobLand, Pierce Brosnan and Helen Mirren head up the Harrigans, an Irish crime family at war on several fronts—and no one knows it better than Hardy. He plays Harry Da Souza, the guy they call when things go wrong, which seems to happen fairly often. Costume designer Rebecca Hale gave him a wardrobe for the job: a battered wax jacket when he’s taking care of the Cotswolds estate, say, or a big overcoat when he’s doing business in the city. There’s one exception to the low-key menswear staples, though: his watch. In season one, he wore a solid gold Rolex GMT-Master II, which is reportedly the most valuable watch in the show. Pretty good for the hired help.

MobLand isn’t Hardy’s first Guy Ritchie gangster part. In RocknRolla, he’s Handsome Bob, the Wild Bunch’s getaway driver. And while everyone else looks like they’d had someone ‘”taken care of” before lunch, Hardy’s low-key menswear staples make him look more like the boy next door. A faded denim shirt. A classic bomber with jeans. A blue checked Western shirt, open just far enough. All simple pieces that let his charm do its thing, which it does when he sweet-talks a lawyer into revealing who’s been grassing to the police. Four years earlier, he played Clarkie in Layer Cake, another member of a London crime crew but one with a very different wardrobe: slicked hair, blazers, open shirts, brandy, and cigars. Similar JD, just with a very different wardrobe.

#6975. Convergence of AI, Quantum Risks Is Impacting Cybersecurity

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures. The 2026 Thales Data Threat Report: Quantum & AI Trends, based on responses from 3,120 security and IT management professionals across 20 countries, found that 98% are considering how the two technologies affect each other. Thirty percent reported a significant increase in security budgets specifically for AI.

The research, conducted by 451 Research by S&P Global, examines both the risks and potential benefits of the technologies. Fifty-seven percent believe quantum computing will enhance machine learning, while 54% expect it to enable advanced simulations of complex systems. The security findings span data governance, cloud infrastructure, and preparations for quantum-resistant encryption.

Cloud-based storage, cloud-delivered applications, and cloud management infrastructure were the three most frequently cited asset types targeted by attackers, at 35%, 34%, and 32%, respectively. The report connects that exposure to the large datasets required for AI training and the rapid expansion of AI infrastructure.

Organizations are also looking to cloud providers for protection: 67% reported investing in their cloud provider's AI-specific security tools, compared with 63% using an established security provider. Respondents could identify multiple sources of protection, including startups, large language model providers, and internally developed controls.

Encryption coverage remains incomplete. Only 7% of organizations reported encrypting more than 80% of their sensitive cloud data, while 29% reported encrypting more than 60%. The report's chart puts the average share encrypted at 47% in 2026, down from 51% in 2025.

The findings extend concerns raised in coverage of Thales' 2025 cloud security research, when AI security already ranked second to cloud security in spending priorities and 52% of respondents reported that AI security spending was displacing existing security budgets. The latest report again places AI security second, while its historical comparison shows average sensitive cloud data encryption coverage falling from 51% in 2025 to 47% in 2026.

AI security was the second-highest prioritized security spending category overall, behind cloud security. But protecting data ranked much lower among the measures organizations use to judge AI projects: Reducing or managing risks of data loss or noncompliance placed sixth among seven success criteria, at 35%.

Improving customer experience led those measures at 68%, followed by reducing employee toil through greater task automation at 63%. Meanwhile, poor data quality or initial data governance was the leading inhibitor to AI adoption, cited by 65%; security risks from exposed data followed at 61%. Another 51% cited AI initiatives moving too quickly to be properly secured.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

Ransomware attacks are becoming more automated, and AI could take that much further, according to cybersecurity company NCC Group's July 2026 threat intelligence report.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

#6969. Flashpoint Receives Patent for Ransomware Risk Model, Tying Vulnerabilites to Likelihood of Attack

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

The patent comes at a time when security teams are confronting a growing vulnerability problem: Flashpoint's Global Threat Intelligence Report: 2026 Midyear Edition tracked 21,667 vulnerability disclosures during the first half of 2026, an 8% increase.

Of those, 4,015, or around 19 percent, already had public or functional exploit code. Flashpoint also reported a 45 percent increase in Ransomware-as-a-Service (RaaS) attacks during the period.

The patent was granted August 11 2026, although the underlying Ransomware Risk model isn't new. It has been available through Flashpoint Vulnerability Intelligence since 2022.

The model addresses a basic vulnerability-management problem: severity and real-world ransomware risk aren't necessarily the same thing, because severity scores do not necessarily indicate which flaws attackers are most likely to target.

The Common Vulnerability Scoring System, or CVSS, measures how severe a vulnerability could be. The Exploit Prediction Scoring System, or EPSS, estimates the probability of exploitation more broadly.

Flashpoint's Ransomware Risk model adds another dimension by comparing newly disclosed vulnerabilities with the characteristics of flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks.

That could change where a vulnerability lands in an organization's patching queue. A lower-severity flaw might normally sit behind numerous critical vulnerabilities. But if its characteristics closely resemble vulnerabilities repeatedly targeted by ransomware operators, security teams may have reason to move it higher.

"A severity score alone can't tell you which vulnerabilities pose the greatest real-world risk of exploitation by ransomware actors," Flashpoint CEO Josh Lefkowitz said. "Our patented Ransomware Risk model applies years of threat intelligence to that problem, giving customers an earlier signal and helping them prioritize based on how attackers actually operate."

The firm's midyear research also illustrates the scale of the prioritization problem. Its vulnerability intelligence covers traditional IT systems as well as cloud, IoT, operational technology, open source software. and third-party libraries.

The company says it also tracks more than 105,000 vulnerabilities that aren't represented in CVE or the National Vulnerability Database, expanding the pool of flaws that security teams may need to evaluate.

The cybersecurity company's patented methodology profiles vulnerabilities using more than 60 technical characteristics, including whether a flaw can be exploited remotely without authentication, whether it affects operational technology systems, and how exploitation could affect data availability.

Those characteristics form a multi-factor fingerprint. The model maps each vulnerability against clusters containing flaws previously exploited in ransomware attacks and assigns a Low, Medium, High, or Critical Ransomware Risk rating.

Flashpoint says the rating can be generated when a vulnerability is disclosed, rather than requiring security teams to wait for public evidence of active exploitation. Ratings are then updated as new ransomware activity and threat intelligence emerge.

Within Flashpoint Ignite, Ransomware Risk appears alongside CVSS, EPSS, Social Risk, and exploit maturity. The idea isn't to replace existing scoring systems, but to give administrators additional information when deciding what to remediate first.

Nor does a High or Critical Ransomware Risk rating mean a vulnerability will necessarily be exploited. The model measures how closely its characteristics resemble vulnerabilities historically associated with ransomware attacks.

For security teams facing thousands of potential vulnerabilities, that distinction matters. The flaw carrying the highest severity score isn't necessarily the one that most closely resembles what ransomware operators have historically chosen to attack.

"Despite being the most popular indicator for priority, we know that severity alone doesn't tell you whether a vulnerability will be exploited or used in ransomware," said Ben Haynes, Data Science & Analytics Lead at Flashpoint and co-inventor of the patented methodology.

"A vulnerability can score low on a traditional severity scale and still share the characteristics we repeatedly see in ransomware attacks."

For more information, go to the Flashpoint site.

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

Our nation's largest early childhood program has not fully caught up with what science now tells us about how children learn.

A recent Cloud Security Alliance report urges organizations to inventory, govern, and continuously monitor machine and agent identities.

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures.

#6968. How to Read ‘The Hunger Games’ Books in Order of Release

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

In the mood to read some Hunger Games books after seeing Sunrise on the Reaping in theaters? Maybe you never visited Panem on the page in the first place. Here’s your guide to how to read Suzanne Collins’s dystopian young adult book series in order. (Remember, even though there are six movies, there are only five books.)

Collins’s trilogy was published in a timely succession back in the day. Then a decade passed, and all of a sudden the author surprised us with a new installment. Are there more Hunger Games books on the horizon? We certainly hope so. But for now, let’s be grateful for what we have.

In the world of young adult fiction, “what if you had to fight to the death with the boy in your class who has a crush on you” is about as perfect as premises get. The emotional stakes are as high as the external stakes. Katniss Everdeen was a survivalist before she volunteered to replace her younger sister in her dystopian society’s televised child slaughter. She could hunt and forage better than most of the people in her Appalachian hometown. What she didn’t know how to do was smile for the cameras and perform for the crowd if that’s what it takes to survive.

Katniss returns to District 12 in a PR relationship with her fellow tribute, victor, and ally Peeta Mellark. They’re miserable on the victory tour, unable to process their feelings and seeing the consequences of their actions everywhere. Then, a year later, President Snow announces that the third Quarter Quell will be Hunger Games: All Stars. The tributes are all former winners and adults. Are Katniss and Peeta simply back where they started? Not really. This time they have allies, and plans to finally move against the Capitol.

In the final book of the original trilogy, Katniss is being dressed up and manipulated for the cameras yet again. But it’s not the Capitol doing it this time. It’s the rebellion, led by the secret District 13. It’s scary to think about how even if you believe in the cause, you may find yourself using the same methods as your enemy. War has broken out in Panem, and Katniss and Peeta are separated. So much happens in this book that they had to split it into two movies.

Ten years later, Collins returned with a fascinating subject for a new prequel: the 10th Hunger Games, during which a teenaged Coriolanus Snow mentored District 12’s lost victor, Lucy Gray Baird, and fell in love with her while brainstorming how to get the murder reality show better ratings with his classmates. Their romance is not really one that you can root for, because we know we can’t fix him. He had bad vibes all along. But it’s a fascinating, twisted tale of how the Hunger Games as we know it came to be.

Next, of course, Collins answered fandom prayers by writing a Haymitch Abernathy prequel. We now know the story of every single District 12 victor’s games. Background details link District 12 women like Lucy Gray and Maysilee Donner to Katniss Everdeen in fun ways. It also serves as a nice companion to Catching Fire, with many appearances from the victors Katniss and Peeta meet during their own Quarter Quell. But be careful what you wish for, because this is a brutal and bloody book about the many ways the Capitol can edit and control your story using propaganda.

The party line is that Suzanne Collins writes when she is inspired to write, and not a second before. As of the release of the film adaptation of Sunrise on the Reaping, there has not been a new book announcement. But that hasn’t stopped the theories.

In the fandom, the character who probably has the most support in this realm is Finnick Odair. He’s sexy, confident, and has a tragically beautiful love story with Annie Cresta, a fellow victor from his district. They were also Career tributes. The districts that chose to buy into the Hunger Games by training children to kill haven’t been explored in depth in the books. There’s definitely potential there for a socially relevant story. What is that process like from the inside? Do they have stage moms? I am all for this, but just have one note: let’s tell it from Annie’s POV.

But there are so many other avenues that a future Hunger Games book could take. Collins could write about Cinna and the world of the stylists. That could include an update on President Snow’s cousin Tigris. She could leave the games behind and write a book about District 13. (Alma Coin origin story, divas?) What about the unknown, presumably dead victor of the first Quarter Quell? There’s also the Dark Days and the first rebel uprising in Panem. I wouldn’t mind a Plutarch story, as well...but I really think we need to hear from a female character this time.

#6966. SailPoint Intros New Capabilities to Address Agentic AI Identity Security Challenge

AI agents are moving from experiments into everyday enterprise environments, but the security controls around them are not keeping pace, according to enterprise identity security company SailPoint. In its latest Horizons of Identity Security report, SailPoint found that 79% of enterprises already have AI agents running in production, while just 2% have deployed identity security tools specifically designed to govern them.

That gap could become harder to ignore as organizations give agents more autonomy. Agents can increasingly access applications, sensitive data, and credentials, and in some cases take actions on a user's behalf, effectively creating a new class of identity that IT and security teams need to manage.

SailPoint, which develops identity security and access governance technology for enterprises, is responding with a collection of new identity security capabilities announced at its Navigate 2026 conference. The company is expanding its Agentic Fabric to discover "shadow AI" agents and provide runtime authorization and controls capable of immediately disabling an agent.

The company also introduced what it calls SailPoint Autonomous Agents, specialized AI agents designed to govern other agents. The security agents can monitor runtime activity, intercept threats, and adjust permissions while allowing legitimate agents to continue operating, SailPoint said.

The idea reflects one of the challenges created by increasingly autonomous AI systems: Traditional human approval processes may not be fast enough to govern software capable of taking large numbers of actions on its own.

"The idea that a security admin is going to review and approve access for a group of autonomous agents making 10,000 tool calls a second isn't just outdated — it's a fantasy," SailPoint CTO Chandra Gnanasambandam said.

Another part of SailPoint's approach focuses on visibility.

New endpoint and browser sensors, along with SIEM and XDR telemetry and non-human identity discovery capabilities, are designed to uncover agents, MCP servers, credentials, tools, and data stores that haven't been brought under an organization's identity program.

The company is also introducing Autonomous Identity Security Posture Management, or A-ISPM, which uses identity information from its Atlas platform to identify dormant accounts, orphaned access, shadow administrators, and other potentially risky access paths. Organizations can then revoke access, disable an identity or AI agent, assign an owner or automate enforcement.

Just-in-Time provisioning is another part of the strategy. Rather than giving people, service accounts, and AI agents permanent access, permissions can be granted when needed and subjected to conditions such as reauthentication or a business justification.

Capabilities across SailPoint's Agentic Fabric, Human Fabric, and Atlas platforms are beginning to roll out globally. A-ISPM and several other announced features are expected in the company's fourth quarter of fiscal 2027.

"Autonomous agents are no longer just experimental copilots; they are becoming the primary execution layer of modern enterprises," SailPoint President Matt Mills said.

For more information, go to the SailPoint site.

#6959. Survey: Most Organizations Are Not Consistently Measuring AI Value Against Costs

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs. The company's Global AI Pulse Q3 2026 report found that 89% of organizations report measurable value from AI, while just 12% consistently assess that value against costs across the organization.

Average planned AI investment for the next 12 months has risen to $210 million, compared with $186 million in the first quarter of 2026.

The findings are based on responses from 2,131 senior leaders across 20 countries and jurisdictions, surveyed between July 23 and August 26, 2026.

Most organizations have established ways to track AI spending: 61% review costs during project approval, and 59% monitor them once systems are operating.

Consistently connecting those costs to value remains less common. Beyond the 12% that do so across the organization, another 26% assess value against costs for most initiatives.

Organizations reporting established AI returns have stronger measurement practices. Among this group, 48% consistently assess value against costs, four times the proportion in the overall survey group.

Cost-monitoring dashboards are also more common among organizations reporting established returns: 77% use them, compared with 43% of those still experimenting.

"The organizations seeing a return did the implementation and the transformation properly: guardrails in place, workforce ready, data cleaned up," said Priya Emmanuel, global head of aIQ at KPMG International. "That is a sequence, not a secret."

Organizations are also developing more formal systems to manage AI deployment.

Fifty-five percent operate a formal AI management layer, which KPMG calls a "harness layer," comprising controls and tools between AI models and business applications.

Security and identity controls, along with data access controls, were the most common capabilities, each reported by 43% of respondents. Model routing, which determines which model handles a particular task, was reported by 23%.

Meanwhile, 53% assign accountability for AI-informed decisions to a named executive, the CEO, or an executive committee.

The proportion reporting significant employee adoption of AI agents rose from 25% in the first quarter to 34% in the third quarter.

Cybersecurity concerns were more common among organizations with mature AI programs.

Half of those reporting established AI returns identified cybersecurity as a barrier, compared with roughly one-third of those still experimenting.

Overall, 86% of respondents said their organizations are adapting their cybersecurity operating models to address AI-accelerated threats.

The report identifies accountability, operational resilience, and cost management as priorities as AI becomes more embedded in business operations.

"Many organizations have made AI models widely available and track their use," said Swaminathan Chandrasekaran, head of the US AI Center of Excellence at KPMG. "But consumption alone is not a strategy."

For the full report, visit the KPMG site.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

Ransomware attacks are becoming more automated, and AI could take that much further, according to cybersecurity company NCC Group's July 2026 threat intelligence report.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

#6957. I'm a Mom of Two, and This Ernesta Rug Gives Me That Luxe Look With the Easy Cleanup of a Washable Option

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

I have to admit, I have a bit of a gripe with washable rugs. For years, the design industry has been trying to convince us that the answer to a beautiful, livable home is a washable rug. And while I understand the appeal, especially as a mom to two young kids, a dog, and a household that sees its fair share of spills, muddy shoes, and general chaos, I’ve just never been entirely sold on the idea.

While washable rug options have definitely improved over the years, there’s still something about the weight, texture, and character of a traditional rug that I find hard to forgo for the sake of washability. I want a rug that feels substantial underfoot, looks better with age, and doesn’t require me to wrestle an eight-by-ten-foot piece of fabric into my washing machine every time someone spills a juice box.

When I learned about Ernesta’s protective rug treatment, I was intrigued. The custom rug brand offers an optional treatment designed to help repel spills and make everyday messes easier to clean, without requiring you to compromise on your rug’s style, material, or size. In theory, it offers the best of both worlds: the look and feel of a beautifully made rug, with a little extra insurance against the realities of family life. Plus, it’s a treatment that’s typically hard to access on your own without going custom through a designer.

I actually have several Ernesta rugs in my home (I’m a huge fan of their quality and approachable customization options!), but after swapping in an Ernesta rug treated with the brand’s protective finish in our living room, I don’t think I’ll ever go back to ordering a rug without it. Here’s what you should know about the treatment, how it's held up in my own home, and why I think it may be a smarter investment than yet another washable rug.

Best for: This rug is an excellent option for families, pet owners, or anyone who just prefers not to have to be too precious with their things.

Not ideal for: Anyone looking for a quick-ship rug option.

Bottom Line: The customization of Ernesta’s rug assortment makes it easy to get the exact look and function that serves your home best, including different sizes, non-slip bases, and protections.

One of the reasons I love Ernesta so much is how approachable they make the ordering process. There’s no visiting a showroom or making 400 phone calls to try to get the right custom-cut size. You simply log on to their site, browse the options, then choose up to five samples to try for just $10.

My samples came incredibly quickly—within a few days, they were at my doorstep, ready for review. Because I already liked the look of the rug we had in our living room (more on that later), I knew I didn’t want to stray too far from the neutral, functional foundation it gave us.

When we started, we had Ernesta’s Emerson sisal rug in the space. The nubby texture and color looked beautiful, but it was a bit small (it was originally sized to an old home that we have since moved from), and the deeper grooves of the weave made it hard to clean up spills. I have two young kids and a dog, and truly not a day goes by where someone hasn’t spilled yogurt or cracker crumbs or juice on the rug.

Still, I don’t think you should have to sacrifice beauty for function in a family home, and Ernesta helped us find that balance. After debating between a few different styles, I ended up settling on the Cally wool flatweave rug in Camel. Wool flatweave rugs are notoriously durable on their own, and I knew the protective coating would only help it hold up better. Bonus: The tighter weave meant fewer nooks and crannies for gunk to get into.

Once we decided on our new style, we measured out the size we’d need, which ended up being around 13 x 15 feet. I loved how easy it was to order the exact size we needed for our space, especially given that it wasn’t a standard measurement. If you’re unsure about the right size for your rug (or how many legs of your furniture should be on the rug at one time), Ernesta has helpful guidance on their site.

Placing the order was easy; you can opt into the protective treatment before adding the order to your cart, as well as choose other details, like the type of edge binding and whether you’d like to include a rug pad (we chose the attached version).

Protective treatments for rugs are nothing new; they’ve been available to design industry insiders for years. That said, very few DTC companies offer the treatment, unless you’re buying through a designer or want to seek out a supplier on your own.

That’s what’s so great about Ernesta’s offerings—it’s literally as easy as checking a box before you add your rug to cart. The treatment starts at $62 and increases depending on the size of your rug. Their formula is non-toxic and works by acting as a protective layer between the surface of the rug and any spills or debris, preventing stains from setting in and keeping the fibers intact for longer.

Receiving and installing our Ernesta rug couldn’t have been easier, especially considering its large size. It was delivered and placed in our entryway, where it waited for a few days until my husband and I had a spare moment to lay it out.

The rug was packaged beautifully, with thick plastic wrapping protecting the roll, a firm cardboard tube at the center, and fabric wrapping the edges so the binding didn’t get roughed up in transit. I thought this was a particularly nice touch.

As with most rugs, the corners were a bit flared when we first unrolled it, so here’s my favorite tip: add several ice cubes to any corner you want to lay flat and let them melt. The slow dissipation of moisture will relax the rug's fibers, and by the time it has melted away completely, you should have a totally flat rug.

I was immediately impressed by the look and feel of this rug—the wool was so soft and plush, and the rubber mat integrated on the bottom meant it did not budge once we laid it down (though it did make it difficult to reposition when we laid it a little wonky initially, word to the wise). There was no “chemical” smell from the protective treatments, and the fibers still felt just as soft as they did on the sample I received before ordering.

Aesthetically, I love the vibe that this rug brings to the space. It’s soft and plush and cozy, and finally in better proportion with the rest of the room—which is great, because I have a pair of armchairs I won at auction waiting in the wings for their moment. Before, they were floating off the rug and looked odd, but now, they’ll be perfect.

Logically, the protective treatment was 100 percent the right choice for our family. Not even five minutes after laying it out, my three-year-old squished an applesauce pouch on the rug, and it came up with ease. In the month since having it, we’ve definitely put this rug through the paces of family life, and it still looks as good as the day we opened it. Cleaning it is easy, and Ernesta has a whole page on their site dedicated to care instructions, depending on the material your rug is made of.

Overall, this is the ideal solution to me: a rug that looks as good as it performs, and doesn’t require any design sacrifice (or yelling from mom) in the process.

#6956. This Forgotten ’90s Comedy Is the Most Demented Thanksgiving Movie Ever

Ed O’Neill and Ethan Embry go on the road in Dutch but end up in some bizarre and disturbing places.

Let’s start off by acknowledging that the late John Hughes wrote and directed the greatest of all Thanksgiving movies with Planes, Trains and Automobiles. There are definitely other great ones, but … Dutch is a different kind of creature. This is a Thanksgiving family comedy in which a grown man fights, kidnaps, and abandons a child. Occasionally he holds him at gunpoint. It’s a rotten child but a kid nonetheless. The movie still made me laugh, but it was mostly in disbelief.

This road-trip saga that Hughes penned and produced in 1991 is by the numbers in most ways but excels in its brazen wrongness, even by the free-range standards of Generation X. I come here to praise this messy movie, not to bury it. It’s a relic from when films could be wilder, shaggier, and more shocking than the rigidly tame family comedies of today. Dutch is many things, but it is not refined to baby-food levels of inoffensive smoothness.

Hughes didn’t direct this one, handing off those duties to Peter Faiman, who had worked extensively in Australian TV and only made one other feature film: 1986’s Crocodile Dundee. I can’t help but feel this was an experiment gone wrong, or maybe a first draft that needed another pass from Hughes. It’s built out of leftovers from better road-trip movies, and like any Frankenstein’s monster, this one runs straight into a wall and ultimately falls apart.

But if you want something different for your Thanksgiving watch, go find this one. Fair warning: It’s not streaming anywhere in the United States, so you’ll have to seek it out on a sketchy Russian bootleg site that makes it look like the footage was ripped from a worn-down VHS.

Dutch stars Ed O’Neill as the title character, a blue-collar guy sent to pick up his girlfriend’s blue-blooded son from a Georgia boarding school and deliver him to Chicago in time for the holiday feast. The not-so-surprising catch is that the kid (played by a 13-year-old Ethan Embry) mercilessly despises Dutch, and honestly the feeling is mutual.

Doyle is one of the most hateful kids in comedy history. Even the ginger demon of Problem Child was more cartoonishly mischievous than sociopathic. Embry, who was then credited as Ethan Randall, isn’t playing a troublemaking scamp like the kid in O. Henry’s “The Ransom of Red Chief” or Hughes’s own Home Alone. He’s a smug sociopath with a violent mean streak.

We learn that Dutch is a salt-of-the-earth cousin to O’Neill’s uncouth Al Bundy from Married with Children because he flicks the caviar off his cracker at a fancy party. (“I make it a rule never to eat anything a fish deposits in a riverbed.”) Okay, fair.

Compare that to how we meet Doyle: Another young boy in his boarding school comes to his room to say he has a call on the dormitory’s community phone line. “Someone from any sort of decent background would knock,” Embry’s character hisses. He then corrects the kid’s vocabulary and threatens to report him to the school administrators for mild cursing. So he’s a snob, a prick, and a rat. But even this is within the bounds of film comedy. You could picture an older version of him as a stuck-up villain in Caddyshack or Animal House.

Then there’s another, even more disturbing phone scene when Doyle gets the bad news that his rich father won’t be helping him get out of this road trip with Dutch. He hangs up and stalks past an elderly janitor who is sweeping the hallway, silently kicking over the garbage can to scatter trash across the helpless old man’s feet. There’s no joke. He’s just a son of a bitch.

Doyle would fit in great with the Children of the Corn, except he’d scowl at their shabby farm clothes.

Obviously this kid is going to get some form of comeuppance. When Dutch shows up at his dorm room to start the trip, Doyle beats him with a golf club and shoots him in the testicles with a BB gun. Something about this is much more violent and visceral than the Looney Tunes–style pratfalls and clobberings of Home Alone.

They have a fistfight in the tiny room, and Dutch seizes the weapon. Does he toss it away or break it? No—he uses it himself to hold the kid at gunpoint. We have just crossed beyond the boundaries of comedy, never to return. Dutch then ties the boy up, lashes him to a hockey stick for easier carrying, and stuffs underwear in his mouth to muffle his cries.

You know a movie is going off the rails when watching it makes you feel like you should be testifying against the characters as a witness for the prosecution.

So begins the drive, with Dutch and Doyle doing their best to annoy each other verbally. Then comes a bizarre scene in which Dutch stops for fireworks for some reason and sets them off in a snowy field while the kid watches from the car. I suppose he’s trying to inspire a little playfulness in the boy, but it just seems like Dutch has lost it and is indulging a pyromaniac streak.

It goes wrong and he sets his coat on fire, which makes it harder to understand why he loses his temper when the boy flicks the butt of his lit cigar at him. The man just went mano a mano with a whizzing fireworks spinner, but this is what lights his fuse?

Dutch does the reasonable thing. He abandons the boy in the snowy night and tells him to walk the rest of the way to the motel. “About 50 miles down the road!” Fifty miles? Okay, Dutch, I believe we’ve added another felony to the charges.

This isn’t one of those moments where the grown-up pulls over just beyond the tree line and waits for him. Dutch actually goes to the hotel, although he occasionally puts down his paperback and checks the window to see if the boy has made it there alive. When Doyle finally does show up, shivering and wet, Dutch says, “Here’s something you should know about me. I don’t fool around. You piss me off, I react.”

Uncle Buck certainly had its off-color and irresponsible moments, but even that havoc-prone babysitter might have called Child Protective Services over this situation.

Doyle responds by stealing Dutch’s car keys while he sleeps and parking the sedan sideways in the snowy road, where it is slammed into by an 18-wheeler. This is a comedy, so the hapless truck driver is not killed on impact.

Faiman shoots all of this with too much gritty realism. Maybe it would work if it was all a little zanier and over the top (think of Vacation), but Dutch just plays less like a series of set pieces and more like an array of crime scenes. They don’t call anyone for help because Dutch doesn’t want to admit to his girlfriend how badly things are going, so they hitchhike the rest of the way and stay in a homeless shelter, where the rotten kid learns that a lot of people have it much worse than he does.

At one point, they are picked up by a pair of pretty young women who are driving home from a job at the Superdome in New Orleans. “We’re call girls,” one of them explains. Hoo boy. Here we go.

But thankfully—mercifully—Dutch goes no further than showing the kid sleeping in the backseat with his head resting on the chest of one of the women. Not great, but it could be worse. (Worse is when Dutch tries to bond with the child over a deck of naked playing cards.)

Even Dutch seems to recognize that “prostitute road trip” probably isn’t the ideal situation for a young boy. But he explains it in the most vulgar way possible: “You and I are riding in the backseat from now on, because what that missile twister can teach you, you don’t need to learn until you’re in prison.” Then the ladies drive off without them, taking Dutch’s wallet and leaving the pair stranded again.

They spend the next leg of their trip riding in the open concrete pipes in the back of a long-haul truck, but they fall asleep and wake up in a trailer depot where they are roughed up by two security guards. Doyle responds by drawing his BB gun on them and making them believe he is going to blow their heads off.

This same joke was deployed by Chevy Chase in the Hughes-scripted National Lampoon’s Vacation (“Are you kidding, this is a Magnum P.I.”) but here, again, it just plays as psychotic violence rather than absurd humor. “The kid is crazy,” Dutch tells the men as they beg for their lives.

By the time the duo finally get home for the Thanksgiving meal, the hostility between Dutch and the kid has softened into a grudging respect. I know: Surprise! Back at the mother’s house, Dutch physically assaults the boy’s callous father, leaving a mark with his pinkie ring on the jerk’s forehead. Instead of an assault charge, it all ends happily ever after with a callback to an earlier gag: Dutch pulls the BB pistol from his pocket and says it’s time to get the revenge he promised he would one day seek. As the boy’s smile fades, Dutch aims the gun at him—and us (!!!)—as the film cuts to the credits.

Oh, Dutch … you dangerously inappropriate maniac. Don’t ever change. And, well, he doesn’t, although the kid goes through the expected metamorphosis to become a more sensitive and understanding human.

They don’t make ’em like this anymore, as the saying goes, and I actually think that’s a pity. Dutch isn’t all that funny, but neither is toothless humor that’s overly safe. I’d gladly tolerate a few misfires like this if it meant more truly unhinged comedies like we had in the ’80s, ’90s, and early 2000s. Dutch stands as a throwback to when small-scale comedies weren’t afraid to take big swings—even if they occasionally landed against someone’s skull or testicles.

#6955. Report: Cyber Attacks Increasingly Exploit Trusted Workplace Tools

In its latest threat intelligence report, Ontinue found that attackers are increasingly using trusted workplace tools to trick users into giving them access to enterprise networks. Approximately 30% of organizations monitored by the cybersecurity company experienced at least one attempt to impersonate IT support through Microsoft Teams during the first half of 2026. About one in every 990 external Teams contacts showed signs of someone pretending to be an internal IT worker.

Ontinue's report says the trouble is that these attacks blend in with ordinary workday communication, which makes them harder to catch.

"The majority of effective attacks today don't rely on sophisticated exploits. They rely on trust," Balazs Greksza, director of Advanced Threat Operations at Ontinue, said in the company's announcement.

Ontinue broke down how a typical attack works: An employee gets an unexpected Teams message or call from someone claiming to work in the organization's IT department. The person warns of a security issue, a software problem or an update that can't wait. Then they walk the employee through opening Windows Quick Assist, installing remote-access software, or running a PowerShell command. Following those steps can hand the attacker control of the employee's computer, often without the employee realizing it.

The approach is very similar to the Black Basta ransomware group, which helped popularize similar attacks in 2024. Unfortunately, Ontinue found the technique has since been adopted by other cybercriminal groups, including some with no known link to Black Basta.

Some threat agents are also combining tactics. They might first overwhelm an employee's inbox with unwanted e-mails, then reach out through Teams pretending to be IT support offering to fix the problem.

"Phishing no longer resides solely in your inbox. It has spread across the entire corporate communications environment," said Mika Aalto, co-founder and CEO of Hoxhunt.

Teams impersonation is only part of the problem. Ontinue also found attackers taking advantage of legitimate Microsoft sign-in processes. Between May and June, researchers identified 10 confirmed device-code phishing compromises across five customer tenants.

In these attacks, criminals trick employees into completing what appears to be a normal Microsoft authentication process. But doing so can give the attacker access to the employee's account and system without actually breaking their password.

The report also found attackers using blockchain-based infrastructure to hide malicious activity, accounting for more than 25% of confirmed malicious ClickFix findings.

"Attackers are no longer looking for gaps in your perimeter. They are looking for the systems your organization already trusts and finding ways to move through them undetected," said Shane Barney, chief information security officer at Keeper Security.

The security firm says that IT needs to keep a closer eye on unexpected Teams contacts, remote-support requests, suspicious PowerShell activity, and changes to authentication settings. Employees should also confirm sensitive requests through established internal channels, rather than relying on the name or account that appears on their screen.

Aviv Nahum, co-founder and CEO of Above Security, noted that AI is making these attacks more convincing by helping criminals imitate familiar voices, writing styles, and conversations.

"The security model therefore must shift from 'does this look real?' to 'can I independently verify that this person is who they claim to be?'" Nahum said.

The full Ontinue report can be found here.

A recent Cloud Security Alliance report urges organizations to inventory, govern, and continuously monitor machine and agent identities.

Recent research from Thales highlights how AI adoption and emerging quantum computing threats are putting pressure on existing security measures.

#6954. All the Details on ‘Phony’ Season 2 for Everyone Obsessed

It’s been a moment since a show’s had me more locked in than Phony on Hulu, and with just one more episode left, inquiring minds (me) want to know: WHEN IS SEASON 2? And also important: is there even going to be a season 2?

Phony follows high schooler Sonny Cooke, who wakes up in the hospital after a car accident to find that his mom seems...off. For starters, her facial features are ever-so-slightly different—but there’s also the fact that she doesn’t remember their special way of saying “I love you” (three hand squeezes), and the fact that she’s just acting generally weird. Was she swapped out by an imposter? Does Sonny have Capgras syndrome? Is there some sort of alternate reality happening? Hopefully we’ll find out by the end of season 1, but then what? Which brings us to...

Not yet. But given how freely the cast and creators are talking about a second season (and the fact that the show is pretty popular), it seems likely to get a sophomore pickup.

Yes...but it’ll also end on a cliffhanger. Creator Nick Paley told Us Weekly “I’m really excited about the cathartic emotionality of the [season 1] cliffhanger. This thing kicks us into the next story in this world because I don’t like constantly depriving people of information. I want this to feel like it means a lot and it feels very important to the characters emotionally. So that cliffhanger does both. Every episode has very big turns at the end. When you’re telling a mystery, it’s really important to show it to people who don’t know.”

Paley told the Los Angeles Times that Phony Season 2 will feature “a whole new story” after the season 1 finale delivers “resolutions and answers.”

The majority of Phony Season 1 was filmed from March through May of 2026, and it premiered in October—a pretty short production time. So, if the show follows a similar schedule for season 2 and starts filming this spring, we could be looking at another October premiere.

Potentially a LOT. Connie Britton, who plays Ellen, told The Wrap, "I think Nick has this thing laid out through probably five or six seasons. He's so clear about what the story is, and so I would be very surprised if he has doesn't have Season 2 all ready to go."

#6951. Blade Runner 2099 Is a Beautiful Slog Through a Grim Future

The new Prime Video series picks up way, way after Denis Villeneuve’s sequel. Not that it matters.

Crowded streets. Long, cool coats. Flying cars darting through smog and neon skyscrapers. An abundance of Japanese aesthetics that imply America’s lost superpower status. Ridley Scott’s seminal 1982 masterwork Blade Runner (itself adapted from Philip K. Dick’s Do Androids Dream of Electric Sheep?) laid the foundation for almost all of cyberpunk science fiction, so much so that everything else feels undeservedly derivative. Even a new Blade Runner. The new series Blade Runner 2099 looks the part, but it lacks the soul of its predecessors, leaving its perspectives on what it means to be more human than human hollow, like a Replicant without memories of its own.

Streaming now on Prime Video (and just in time for a long holiday weekend) is Blade Runner 2099. A stand-alone continuation of both Ridley Scott’s towering original and Denis Villeneuve’s remarkable legacy sequel, the series takes place in 2099 (decades after Villeneuve’s Blade Runner 2049). An unseen revolution has elevated Replicants to the ruling class of Los Angeles, rendering flesh-and-bone meat-bag humans as the new discriminated minority.

Hunter Schafer flexes some of her budding leading-lady chops as Cora, a real human posing as a Replicant to smuggle herself and her brother out of Los Angeles. Her ruse leads her to Olwen (Oscar-winner Michelle Yeoh), a Replicant Blade Runner spending the final days of her artificial life span seeking out someone from her past. A humorless buddy-cop mystery, Blade Runner 2099 is mired in a plot-centric narrative that cares less about the why of all things—an underrated and beautiful feature of its predecessors—than the what and how.

In other words, it’s the first time a piece of Blade Runner media actually zeroes in on the plot, instead of anything else more interesting. Try not to think of this as an eight-hour Blade Runner series but rather an eight-hour series stretching at the seams to feel worthy of its branded title.

Is it because streaming TV in 2026 has become so plastic and hollow that even Blade Runner 2099 feels like an unengaging slog? Or were the direction of both Scott and Villeneuve so immaculate that we were distracted from how turgid they might have been all this time? Regardless, 2099’s lack of auteurship is its most deafening shortcoming. The series has been steered to the screen by executive producer Silka Luisa, whose warmer color palettes—based on her choice of more sunshine, which glistens off surfaces—lend 2099 some identity. Some.

But beyond even that minor distinction, Blade Runner 2099 is still just a plot-y crime drama that checks off boxes and goes through the motions of its genre, with nary a real thing to say. About us, about our fragile humanity, about the demise of all we’ve built through artificial intelligence. Blade Runner 2099 is a dystopian sci-fi in all the ways you expect it, but it’s hardly a good one. Sure, the original movies were oppressive, dreary, and morbid too. But they possessed a knowing glare into our fears about the future and our imminent collapse as a civilized species. The new series lacks this same insight, or indeed any of its own, all in the name of goosing up engagement numbers for the closest thing to a real-life Tyrell Corp. (Do shareholders dream of electric quarterly reports?)

Despite surface-level similarities to the movies, plus a moody noir-like motif with a sexy saxophone that, admittedly, completely and unapologetically rips, the plodding Blade Runner 2099 is a needless walkabout through a world that never begged for one. Needless is a good word, actually. The show’s strong visual execution is negated by its inability to rise to the occasion as a worthwhile or enlightening probe of the human experience, or even just as an entertaining cop show. It may be smart, but what good is intelligence if there isn’t an ounce of grace?

#6950. Sophos Launches Tool for Shadow AI Defense

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos. In a recent product announcement, the company highlighted the growing use of shadow AI, referring to AI tools and agents operating without formal organizational approval or oversight. The security firm cited research indicating that 69% of organizations suspect or have evidence of unauthorized AI use.

Separate research from Gartner and Cloud Security Alliance found that 82% of organizations discovered shadow AI agents in their environments during the previous year.

According to the security firm, organizations have struggled to identify which AI tools employees use, what information those tools access, and whether their use complies with security policies.

"Organizations are adopting AI faster than they can secure it, and most security teams can't answer three basic questions: which AI tools are running, what data is going into them, and whether that activity lines up with policy," said Kyle Falkenhagen, senior vice president of product management at Sophos.

The company also cited an IDC projection that the number of AI agents could reach 1.3 billion by 2028.

Sophos researchers reported observing attackers use AI agents within a customer's network to build and test nearly 80 malware modules.

The cybersecurity firm announced the general availability of Sophos AI Defense, a security offering designed to identify AI tools, monitor their activity, and enforce organizational security policies.

The product operates through Sophos Fusion, the company's cybersecurity platform, and uses information from devices, networks, identity systems, and browsers.

According to the company, AI Defense can inspect prompts, uploads, and other AI interactions to identify potentially risky activity and help prevent sensitive data exposure.

For more information, go to the Sophos site.

In an active social engineering campaign, cyber attackers are impersonating IT help desks and using fake passkey setup requests to compromise employee identities and gain access to enterprise cloud data.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

Ransomware attacks are becoming more automated, and AI could take that much further, according to cybersecurity company NCC Group's July 2026 threat intelligence report.

Avantis Education, the creator of ClassVR, has introduced two new immersive learning libraries for its Eduverse+ platform: EscapeRooms, a math suite for K-8 students, and LingoLab, a Spanish and English language learning suite for students in grades 6-12.

#6949. What Exactly Is an Aura Gap Relationship?

It’s all about matching and complementing energies, and it’s one of my non-negotiable.

When I first heard about the social media concepts of age, height, and swag gaps, I was confused as to how they could possibly threaten established relationships, or generally anyone who was into someone else. After all, these are generally known factors.

When you meet a new love interest, there will always be characteristics that reveal themselves over time—that's what dating is. Whether you meet someone in person or online, you generally know what you're getting into. Sure, you can lie on Hinge, but one IRL date should usually sets the facts straight.

Unless you're Pedro Pascal in Materialists or Demi Moore in The Substance, there's not much you can do about age and height gaps. Swag gaps, on the other hand, are a little more amenable. You can become a guy's stylist, educate him on the lingo, spots, and social niceties needed to play on the same aesthetically-pleasing field. It takes brute force, hard work, and someone willing to give you carte blanche with their time and dime, but it is possible to close that gap.

What is far more dangerous, explosive, and slow-killing is the aura gap. But what exactly is aura? It’s basically the vibe you give off, from your confidence, to your demeanor, to if you’re warm and personable or cold and short. It’s something you’re born with, not something you can buy. Sure, could you learn social skills and etiquette, of course. You could even pay someone to make you seem like you have good aura online, but the truth will eventually present itself in person. You can manufacture an image, but you can't concoct the energy you bring into a room.

When we consider aura-gap relationships and their longevity, I suspect the issue is less about understanding someone's faults and more fundamental to a partnership's long-term compatibility.

The best long-term example I can think of is my parents. My mom is larger-than-life, extroverted, crass, and will leave most people in ab-clenching laughter. My dad is brilliant, detail-oriented, and gets anything and everything done. While those attributes complement each other wonderfully, it's the indescribable warmth and ferocity the duo exude together that makes them such a good team. Their personalities are different, but their auras are entirely compatible.

And I think that's where the distinction lies. An aura gap isn't necessarily about one person being louder, funnier, or more charismatic than the other. It's about whether the energy you bring into a relationship is reciprocated, complemented, or quietly diminished.

Bad habits, political and religious differences, and financial compatibility are all big factors on marriage checklists, but aura, to me, is even bigger. You can agree on where to live, how many children to have, and what constitutes an appropriate amount of money to spend on dinner, but what good is all that if the person sitting across from you makes you feel like a less interesting version of yourself?

As we date, the question should be: does the person opposite me radiate a consistent energy that not only complements my own, but makes mine better? Aura is my non-negotiable, and frankly, it should be yours, too. I'd rather be with someone who makes me feel more myself than someone who makes me feel like I have to be enough for the both of us.

#6946. You Don’t Need Designer Sneakers. You Need These $95 German Army Trainers.

We must admit that these ones from Quince are damn good.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Welcome to the Esquire Endorsement. Heavily researched. Thoroughly vetted. These picks are the best way to spend your hard-earned cash.

In my life, I lean toward luxury and secondhand clothing wherever possible, desperately trying to get away from all the cheap garbage being sold to me on Instagram ads and the Amazon homepage. I’ve spent years of my life hunting sales and eBay listings for clothes and boots. I’m like an edge-habitat pine growing in the direction of the radiant sun that is well-made clothing, trying to forget the fast fashion slop that lies in the forest behind me.

But I throw that out the window when it comes to sneakers. Designer sneakers are, for me, one of the dumbest things I could ever buy. Even when they are made with real leather, you are paying for a plastic sole that has a lifespan of a few years. I don’t think you should pay more than the $200 price of a pair of New Balance when it comes to sneakers.

Under that price point, I’ve never been able to find a good pair German Army Trainers, which I have always wanted. That changed when I found these. Quince (yeah yeah, I was just complaining about fashion slop, but life is full of contradictions) makes a perfect pair of GATs—and it makes them for $95. I honestly think every man in the world should buy a pair of these.

This is my real qualm with designer sneakers, and it totally undercuts anything you could tell me about the second-hand sneaker hype market. What you should pay for, when you pay expensive prices for clothing, is craftsmanship. When you buy a Gucci 1953 loafer or Loro Piana cashmere you probably arrived there because of the brand, but you are paying for the craftsmanship. I see no such correlation in designer sneakers, even the ones those brands make.

But a $95 pair of kicks? That’s honest. Even though I’m not bullish on Quince’s fast fashion take on quiet luxury (nor am I bullish on its caviar program), and the fact that Quince chose to closely replicate the Margiela GAT instead of the original German army models, I will let it slide. At $95, there are no claims being made about leather quality, or the footbed, or whatever is going on in the sole you can’t see. They look good and are easy to replace; it’s about as honest of a deal as you can find shopping these days.

The thing about the original BW Sport GATs—which you can get for about this price still, though used workout sneakers aren’t my cup of tea—is that they’re just about bulletproof. Those original mid-century designs made were first issued in the 1970s and stopped being issued around the end of the Cold War, a real feat of durability.

These shoes mimic that Army-issued durability. I’ve worn these sneakers for going on a year now. In that time, I’ve seen many pairs of more expensive kicks start to fall apart, get scuffed beyond recognition, and stink like hell. These have not. The pair I’ve worn all year is the pair pictured in this story. Nothing I say in this endorsement could be more convincing than that.

And really that’s the argument here. When I wrote about the Chuck Taylors I normally wear, it was a use-it-and-lose-it argument—they cost $45, who cares if the soles get holes within 12 months. But here, I fear I’m going to sound like a marketing mouthpiece for Quince. These shoes are $95, but they’ll last just as long as a pair that cost you $950. That’s all you can ask for these days.

#6944. Microsoft Expands AI Security Platform to Address Agentic AI Security Risks

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

In a company blog post, Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security, outlined how Microsoft plans to use Project Perception to monitor, manage, and contain what it calls "agentic insiders." These are AI agents deployed within an environment that can access data, use software tools, and make decisions without constant human supervision. While they can improve productivity, Microsoft warns that they also introduce security risks, even when they haven't been compromised by an attacker.

"Agents are unpredictable," Gallot wrote. "They will adapt as the context changes, may not behave as intended, and will collaborate and collude with other agents to reach their objectives, without needing to be compromised."

Project Perception was first introduced in July. The platform, which entered public preview Aug. 3, is designed to help security teams respond to increasingly sophisticated cyber threats using AI-powered automation.

Instead of leaning only on traditional security alerts and manual investigations, Project Perception uses three kinds of specialized AI agents working together. Red team agents hunt for possible vulnerabilities, blue team agents look into suspicious activity, and green team agents step in to fix problems and strengthen defenses.

To spot threats and respond faster, the system pulls in security data from identities, endpoints, cloud environments, applications, and other sources.

Now Microsoft wants to use those same capabilities on the AI agents already running inside enterprise networks. Project Perception would find those agents, give them identities and permissions, and keep an eye on what they do across different systems. It would also keep assessing the risks that come with their actions.

Microsoft also described a plan for "Guardian Agents," specialized AI agents that supervise other agents and step in when their behavior gets risky. That might mean limiting an agent's permissions or blocking certain actions before they cause harm.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," Gallot wrote.

The initiative also reflects concerns raised by Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella in an Oct. 10 essay on AI security.

Nadella argued that enterprises should treat powerful AI models much like potential insider security risks, particularly when those models can access confidential information or perform critical business operations.

"We simply can't outsource responsibility for what intelligence does on our behalf," Nadella wrote.

He called for security controls that operate independently of AI models, including restricted permissions, detailed activity logging, and mechanisms that allow administrators to stop an agent's actions.

"In other words, we need to separate the supply of intelligence from the authority over it," Nadella wrote.

Microsoft admits that watching what AI agents do is only part of the answer. Gallot said that future security tools will also need to show why AI models make the choices they do, not just what actions they end up taking.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.
